This is the same treatment as would be the case for the acquisition of a portfolio of assets which is not part of a business combination. • After their original recognition, the subsequent accounting for debt instruments acquired in a business combination is in the scope of IFRS 9. The impairment requirements in IFRS 9.5.5 are part of the subsequent measurement of those debt instruments (see IFRS 9.5.2.1 and 5.2.2 as well as the structure of the standard, i.e., that initial measurement is addressed in IFRS 9.5.1 and subsequent measurement is addressed in IFRS 9.5.2). Consequently, the recognition of an expected credit loss allowance in IFRS 9.5.5 occurs at the first reporting date after the business combination (see IFRS 9.5.5.3 and 5.5.5, which refer to measurement at the reporting date). This results in recognising a corresponding impairment loss in profit or loss (see IFRS 9.5.5.8). Therefore, at the first reporting date after the business combination, following the guidance in IFRS 9, a loss allowance is recognised. This will result in an impairment loss that is recognised in profit or loss (rather than an adjustment to goodwill), just as would be the case if the entity were to originate those assets or acquire them as a portfolio, rather than acquire them through a business combination. The assets will all be measured on the basis of 12-month expected credit losses unless the assets are purchased or originated credit- impaired or the assets have increased significantly in credit risk between the date of the business combination and the first reporting date. How we see it Despite the colloquial reference to a ‘day one’ loss that results from the expected credit loss impairment model in IFRS 9, it is important to understand that the recognition of a loss allowance for newly acquired (whether purchased or originated) debt instruments that are in the scope of the impairment requirements in IFRS 9.5.5 is a matter of subsequent measurement of those financial instruments. This means that the acquirer recognises the loss allowance for all debt instruments acquired in a business combination (that are subject to impairment accounting) in the reporting period that includes the business combination but not as part of that business combination, and with a corresponding impairment loss in profit or loss. As a result, these acquired assets are carried at a value below their fair value by what is often referred to as ‘day two’, when the 12-month expected credit loss is booked. This may seem counter-intuitive to many preparers and users of financial statements, but is clearly what is intended by IFRS 9. Financial instruments: Impairment The only exception to the need to record expected credit losses on day two is the specific accounting for purchased or originated credit-impaired financial assets (see section 3.3 above). This applies to financial assets which are already credit-impaired at the acquisition date. A financial asset is credit-impaired when one or more events that have a detrimental impact on the estimated future cash flows of that financial asset have occurred (see section 3.1 above). Financial assets that are already credit-impaired at the acquisition date are not accounted for under the ‘general approach’ of the impairment model of IFRS 9 but instead follow a different application of the impairment model (see IFRS 9.5.5.13 and 5.5.14). This does not involve recognising the loss allowances for 12-month or lifetime expected credit losses. For such assets, the effective interest rate is determined using a cash flow estimate that includes all initial lifetime expected credit losses and no separate allowance is initially made for expected credit losses, as the expected credit loss is part of the ‘credit-adjusted effective interest rate’. Subsequently, the cash flow estimate used for calculating amortised cost, including expected credit losses, is updated at each reporting date, so that any cumulative changes in lifetime expected credit losses since initial recognition is recognised as a loss allowance and corresponding gain or loss in profit or loss as expectations change. This differs from financial assets that are not purchased or originated credit-impaired, where an allowance must be made for expected credit losses (see sections 3.1 and 3.3 above). It follows that, on a business combination, the acquirer needs to classify the acquired debt instruments that will be recorded at amortised cost or at fair value through other comprehensive income, according to whether they are purchased credit-impaired or not. If not, they will be regarded as stage 1 assets and be subject to a 12-month expected credit loss. If they are purchased credit-impaired, then there will be no need for an additional expected credit loss unless there is a subsequent change in estimated lifetime expected credit loss (see section 3.3 above). Furthermore, these purchased credit-impaired loans will always be treated as such and are never subsequently subject to the staging requirements. Also, none of the acquired assets, either performing or purchased credit-impaired, will be classified as stage 2 or stage 3 at the date of initial recognition. A further issue for debt instruments acquired in a business combination is the application of the rebuttable presumption that there has been a significant increase in credit risk if the loan is 30 days past due (see section 6.2.2 above) and that default does not occur later than when a financial asset is 90 days past due (see section 5.1 above). For example, if loans are acquired two days before the acquirer’s reporting date when they are already 29 days past due or 89 days past due, and if the acquirer were to apply the above rebuttable presumptions, such loans would be presumed to have significantly increased in credit risk (i.e., moved from stage 1 to stage 2) and have defaulted (i.e., moved from stage 1 to stage 3) respectively as at the reporting date. • For the loans that were 29 days past due, arguably, given the heightened credit risk as at the acquisition date, the loans are unlikely to have significantly increased in credit risk over the short period of two days. Moving the loans from stage 1 to stage 2 would not be consistent with the relative measurement approach of the IFRS 9 impairment model, which is intended to track and determine whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition (see section 6 above). However, the acquirer is permitted to rebut the presumption and apply it only to those loans that are not past due when purchased, or else ‘start the clock’ only at the date of acquisition (see section 6.2.2 above for further discussion on the 30 days past due rebuttable presumption). • For the loans that were 89 days past due, similar to the issue for loans that were 29 days past due, moving the loans from stage 1 directly to stage 3 could potentially not result in useful information for the users of the accounts. Acquirers are recommended to consider all the criteria set out in IFRS 9 to determine whether such loans were actually purchased credit-impaired (see section 3.3 above) and not to rely solely on the days past due. 7.5 Interaction between expected credit losses calculations and fair value hedge accounting For a financial asset that becomes credit-impaired, IFRS 9 requires impairment to be measured by reference to the gross carrying amount of the asset, which would include the fair value hedge adjustment. [IFRS 9.B5.5.33]. Otherwise, a part of the asset’s carrying amount would not have a loss allowance or the loss allowance would be overstated (in case of a negative fair value hedge adjustment). The effect of fair value hedge accounting is to adjust the effective interest rate, which affects the rate used to discount expected future cash flows. The rationale for this is that the original interest rate before the hedge becomes irrelevant once the carrying amount of the loan is adjusted for any changes in its fair value attributable to interest rate movements. Therefore, for a credit-impaired financial asset in stage 3, the effective interest rate would be adjusted to reflect any fair value hedge adjustment. Financial instruments: Impairment However, under the expected credit loss impairment model an allowance is required for all assets, i.e., including assets in stages 1 and 2, in addition to assets in stage 3. Hence, if the discount rate were to be adjusted whenever fair value hedge accounting is applied, then all fair value hedge adjustments would need to be taken into account in calculating expected credit losses. This would give rise to significant operational challenges. IFRS 9 is not explicit on this matter, but two points in IFRS 9 would seem to be relevant. First, except for credit-impaired assets, the expected credit loss requirements are not based on an asset’s ‘carrying amount’ but on the contractual cash flows that are expected to be lost. Second, IFRS 9 only requires a fair value hedge adjustment to be amortised when the hedged item ceases to be adjusted for changes in fair value attributable to the risk being hedged, which can be read to imply that until then there is no need to adjust the effective interest rate, and hence the rate used to discount expected credit losses. [IFRS 9.6.5.10]. We believe the requirement is not clear and, so at least until it is clarified potentially as part of the hedge accounting PIR, there is an accounting policy choice for exposures that are the designated hedged items in fair value hedge relationships. One approach would be to adjust the effective interest rate whenever a fair value hedge adjustment is made and hence change the interest rate used to discount expected credit losses. The other approach would be not to adjust the effective interest rate or otherwise take into account the fair value hedge adjustment until the effective interest rate is adjusted to amortise the fair value hedge adjustment. [IFRS 9.6.5.8]. Such an adjustment to the effective interest rate is permitted to commence at any time but would, at the latest, be required when hedge accounting ceases or when the financial asset becomes credit-impaired, i.e., moved to stage 3 (see Chapter 49 at section 6.4.2.B). How we see it Entities have an accounting policy choice for exposures that are the designated hedged items in fair value hedge relationships, i.e., to adjust the effective interest rate whenever a fair value hedge adjustment is made and hence, change the interest rate used to discount expected credit losses, or not to adjust the effective interest rate until the fair value hedge adjustment is required to be amortised. 7.6 Changes in expected credit loss methodologies - errors, changes in estimates or changes in accounting policies Many entities, having now had some experience of applying IFRS 9, may be considering changing various aspects of their expected credit loss methodologies. The potential changes could include the correction of errors found in the model, updating probability of default, loss given default and exposure at default for recent experience, making changes to how significant increases in credit risk are assessed, and amending the definition of default and write-off policies. The question that these potential amendments pose is whether they are accounted for as prior year errors or changes in accounting policies, both of which (if material) would be adjusted for retrospectively, or changes in estimates which would be accounted for as they arise. The majority of such changes are likely to be changes in estimates. The only changes that will likely be treated as errors are those that are ”omissions from, and misstatements in, the entity’s financial statements for one or more prior periods arising from a failure to use, or misuse of, reliable information that: (a) was available when financial statements for those periods were authorised for issue; and (b) could reasonably be expected to have been obtained and taken into account in the preparation and presentation of those financial statements.” [IAS 8.5] Examples of errors would include failure to calculate expected credit losses on all relevant exposures, errors in input data and incorrect spreadsheet formulae. Most amendments to expected credit loss calculation methods, including use of more sophisticated or more up to date data, revisions to formulae or measurement on a more granular basis, are likely to be changes in estimates. It is unlikely that amendments to the calculation approach would be regarded as a change in accounting policy. If changes are made to how credit risk is assessed for the staging assessment, care must be taken to distinguish between changes in how the risk is perceived and how it is measured, and changes in what is regarded as ‘significant’. In contrast, the effect of a change of what is deemed to be ‘significant’ would normally be treated as a change in estimate. Financial instruments: Impairment How we see it In general, it is not appropriate to reassess the credit risk of a financial asset on initial recognition, i.e., the probability of default on origination is not revised if the expected credit loss model is modified or improved. If the initial credit risk turns out to be riskier than expected, then the initial credit risk is not amended; it is possible that the asset will be transferred to stage 2 and the profit or loss impact would be accounted for as a change in estimate. On the other hand, if all that is amended is the way that the credit risk is measured, such as expressing the risk in terms of 12-month probability of defaults as opposed to lifetime probability of defaults, or using a different definition of default, it may be necessary to recalibrate the initial credit risk using the new measurement basis, so as to measure initial and current credit risk on a consistent basis. 7.7 Securitisations and special purpose entities Securitisation transactions are carried out to transfer risk and/or raise finance from external investors by enabling them to invest in parcels of specific financial assets such as mortgage loans, credit card receivables, other consumer loans, or lease receivables. In a relatively simple structure, a special purpose entity issues notes to investors and lends the money raised to an entity (the ‘originator’), secured by the portfolio of assets, either with recourse to the originator if the cash flows on the portfolio are insufficient to repay the loan, or sometimes with no recourse. A typical more complicated structure involves the originator selling the portfolio of loans to the special purpose entity. In either case, the special purpose entity’s equity share capital, which will typically be small, will often be owned by a trustee on behalf of a charitable trust. The loans will often be issued in series of tranches, with varying levels of risk, established through a ‘waterfall’ of payments, and at different rates of interest to reflect the risk. The originator will often continue to administer the loans as before, for which it will receive a service fee from the special purpose entity (see Chapter 48 at section 3.3). Some of the common issues that have arisen in practice in relation to securitisations and special purpose entities include contractually linked instruments and subordinated interests (see section 6.1.2 above), the application by the special purpose entity of the expected credit loss requirements when the transferor is not able to derecognise the portfolio of loans (see section 7.7.1 below), and accounting for a financial liability issued by an special purpose entity (see section 7.7.2 below). 7.7.1 Expected credit loss requirements for the special purpose entity if the transferor is not able to derecognise the portfolio of loans In many securitisation structures, the originator will retain some form of interest in the transferred assets which will preclude derecognition of the assets by the originator. In the simplest types of structures, where the special purpose entity just lends money to the originator, secured on the portfolio, the special purpose entity will recognise this loan and measure expected credit losses accordingly. If the loan is without recourse to the originator, then the collateral value of the portfolio will be the most important element of the calculation. In more complex cases, where the portfolio has been sold by the originator to the special purpose entity, the originator may retain an interest through various different mechanisms. These can include, for example, the provision of guarantees or put options, an obligation to substitute non-preforming loans, deferred consideration, or by the originator investing in a junior tranche of the notes issued by the special purpose entity. In each case, the special purpose entity does not recognise the transferred assets, but recognises a ‘deemed loan’ receivable from the originator for the amount of the proceeds paid to buy the transferred assets (see Chapter 48 at sections 3.3 and 3.6.2). The ‘deemed loan’ is a financial asset which, following a similar approach to the classification of contractually linked instruments (as described in Chapter 44 at section 5.6), will often qualify to be recorded at amortised cost or at fair value through other comprehensive income. Assuming it is not measured at fair value through profit or loss, as described in section 6.1.2 above, it is subject to the expected credit loss requirements of IFRS 9. However, IFRS 9 does not set out how the expected credit losses on the deemed loan are determined and whether the performance of the underlying loans are reflected in the measurement of the deemed loan in the standalone accounts of the special purpose entity. Financial instruments: Impairment The special purpose entity is exposed to two sources of risk on the deemed loan: (1) the credit risk of the originator, in effect collateralised by the portfolio of loans, and (2) an exposure to the underlying portfolio, to the extent that the originator does not retain all the risks. For instance, if the originator indemnifies the special purpose entity for the first 5% of impairment losses, the special purpose entity is exposed to the originator if it is unable to pay out on the indemnity, but also to the underlying loans to the extent that losses exceed 5%. Although the second of these risks is not strictly a credit risk arising on the deemed loan, similar to the treatment of contractually linked instruments (see section 6.1.2 above), both of these sources of risk would be included by the special purpose entity in the measurement of expected credit losses. It follows that, when determining whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk on the deemed loan, depending on the terms of the arrangement, the special purpose entity is likely to need both to look through to the staging of the underlying loans but also to take into account any change in the originator’s credit risk. If the originator retains all the risks associated with the underlying loans, then the determination would only consider the originator’s ability to pay. Some securitisations are structured such that the special purpose entity is exposed to a very low risk of loss. This might be achieved, for instance, through the originator being paid deferred consideration dependent on the amounts realised from the loans or, if the special purpose entity lends to the originator secured by the loans and ensuring that there is sufficient excess collateral to absorb any losses expected to arise. In these circumstances it is possible that the special purpose entity’s expected credit losses may be immaterial. As will be apparent from this brief discussion, securitisation structures vary significantly, and each transaction will need to be assessed based on its own facts and circumstances. Disclosures will also play an important role in providing useful information to the users of the accounts with respect to the securitisation arrangements in place, the exposure to the originator, the credit quality of the underlying loans and how impairment is assessed. 7.7.2 Accounting for a financial liability issued by a special purpose entity In many securitisation structures, a special purpose entity invests in a portfolio of loans and raises funds by issuing listed notes to investors. The special purpose entity’s contractual obligations on the listed notes are linked to the performance of the loans less the interest spread as, typically, the listed notes bear an interest rate slightly lower than the loans to allow the special purpose entity to earn a spread that is sufficient to cover its operating costs and/or to provide some loss absorption capital. The retention of the interest spread and inability of the special purpose entity to pass the cash flows received from the loan to the noteholder ‘without material delay’ usually result in the special purpose entity failing the ‘pass-through’ test for the loans and so the special purpose entity cannot derecognise them (see Chapter 48 at sections 3.6.2 and 3.6.2.D). Assuming that the loans held by the special purpose entity are recorded at amortised cost (or fair value through other comprehensive income), under IFRS 9, the special purpose entity is required to record expected credit losses on the loans. However, any losses suffered on the loans will be borne by the noteholders, due to the contractual linkage feature. This raises the question on how the linkage feature is accounted for. In our view, the linkage feature is accounted for as part of the listed notes liability. In most circumstances, the linkage feature is not a credit derivative that is embedded in a host debt instrument and required to be separated (for instance, because it would allow one party to transfer the credit risk of a particular reference asset, which it may not own, to another party). [IFRS 9.B4.3.5(f)]. If the linkage feature is closely related to the liability (see # Chapter 42 at section 4), it is not separated as an embedded derivative, and is therefore required to be accounted for as part of the amortised cost of the liability. In such instances, any revisions to the estimated contractual cash payments on the listed notes due to the linkage feature is accounted for as an adjustment to the amortised cost of the noteholder liability that is recognised in profit or loss as per IFRS 9.B5.4.6 (see # Chapter 46 at section 3.4.1). How we see it The contractual linkage feature, where any losses suffered on the loans will be borne by the noteholders, is accounted for as part of the listed notes liability. Any revisions to the estimated contractual cash payments on the listed notes due to the linkage feature is accounted for as an adjustment to the amortised cost of the noteholder liability (discounted at the effective interest rate) and recognised in profit or loss. Financial instruments: Impairment Although the linkage feature might be thought of as a financial guarantee of the special purpose entity’s loans, as it has the effect of transferring credit losses on the loans from the special purpose entity to the noteholders, it cannot be accounted for as a credit enhancement of the loans and thereby reduce the expected credit losses. Such a treatment is not consistent with the requirements of IFRS 9 and the IFRS IC discussion that if a credit enhancement is required to be recognised separately by IFRS accounting standards, an entity cannot include the cash flows expected from it in the measurement of expected credit losses (see section 5.8.1.A above). As a consequence of accounting for the linkage feature as part of the listed notes liability, while the special purpose entity will recognise gains on the linkage feature if the loans deteriorate, as it records higher expected credit losses on the loans, the gains recognised under IFRS 9.B5.4.6 are unlikely to match the expected credit loss impairment expense, giving rise to some volatility of earnings. This is because the expected credit losses are calculated based on the probability-weighted amount of credit losses that is based on 12-month expected credit losses if the loans are in stage 1 and lifetime expected credit losses if the loans are in stages 2 and 3, whilst the gains recognised under IFRS 9.B5.4.6 are calculated based on the present value of the estimated future cash receipts. A possible approach to reduce the effect of this accounting mismatch is, on initial recognition, to irrevocably designate the loans and notes at fair value through profit or loss in order to eliminate or significantly reduces the measurement or recognition inconsistency, in accordance with IFRS 9.4.1.5 and 4.2.2 (see # Chapter 44 at section 6). 7.8 Measuring expected credit losses during the coronavirus pandemic 7.8.1 Introduction A major challenge that commenced in 2020 for the application of the IFRS expected credit loss model was the coronavirus pandemic. The virus significantly affected the world economy and also resulted in significant volatility in the financial and commodities markets. Many countries imposed travel bans on millions of people and many more were subject to quarantine measures. Businesses had to deal with lost revenue and disrupted supply chains. This gave rise to liquidity issues for many entities and also had consequential impacts on the credit quality of entities along the supply chain and also had knock on effects on retail loan portfolios (consumer and mortgage loans) as many businesses had to reduce staff numbers. The deterioration in credit quality of loan portfolios and trade receivables, as a result of the pandemic had a significant impact on expected credit loss measurement. In responding to these challenges, certain governments and central banks introduced, or directed or encouraged commercial banks to introduce, various types of relief measures. The consequence was that there were two sources of uncertainty for the calculation of expected credit losses: first, the potential development and consequences of the virus and, second, the extent and effect of the various relief measures. Whilst the coronavirus pandemic has largely abated and does not pose the same health and macroeconomic threat now, it is still worth considering the guidance issued by the IASB, governments and regulators in response to the uncertainty around the calculation of ECL caused by the crisis as the principles of the guidance provided may be applicable to other macroeconomic phenomena that could arise in future. 7.8.2 Guidance A number of prudential and securities regulators, including the European Banking Authority, the European Central Bank, the European Securities and Market Authority, the Prudential Regulation Authority in the United Kingdom and The Office of the Superintendent of Financial Institutions in Canada (the regulators) published guidance on the regulatory and accounting implications of the pandemic. The IASB closely engaged with many prudential and securities regulators and others regarding the application of IFRS 9 in the context of the coronavirus pandemic. On 27 March 2020, the IASB published a document for educational purposes, IFRS 9 and covid-19 - Accounting for expected credit losses applying IFRS 9 Financial Instruments in the light of current uncertainty resulting from the covid-19 pandemic. The document was broadly consistent with the guidance from the regulators and emphasised the following: • IFRS 9 requires the application of judgement and both requires and allows entities to adjust their approach to determining expected credit losses in different circumstances. • To assess significant increases in credit risks, IFRS 9 requires that entities assess changes in the risk of a default occurring over the expected life of a financial instrument. Both the assessment of significant increases in credit risk and the measurement of expected credit losses are required to be based on reasonable and supportable information that is available to an entity without undue cost or effort (see section 5.9.5 above). Financial instruments: Impairment • Entities were required to develop estimates based on the best available information about past events, current conditions and forecasts of economic conditions. In assessing forecast conditions, consideration should have been given both to the effects of coronavirus and the significant government support measures being undertaken (see sections 5.9.3, 5.9.5, and 5.8.1.C above). • It was likely to be difficult at this time (March 2020) to incorporate the specific effects of coronavirus and government support measures on a reasonable and supportable basis. However, changes in economic conditions should be reflected in macroeconomic scenarios and if the effects of coronavirus could not be reflected in models, post-model overlays or adjustments would need to be considered • Although circumstances at the time were difficult and created high levels of uncertainty, if expected credit loss estimates were based on reasonable and supportable information and IFRS 9 was not applied mechanistically, useful information could be provided about expected credit losses. The European Central Bank also issued on 1 April 2020 IFRS 9 in the context of the coronavirus (covid-19) pandemic that emphasised the importance of identifying and reporting asset quality deterioration. Subsequently, on 4 December 2020, the European Central Bank issued a letter to the CEO of banks Identification and measurement of credit risk in the context of the coronavirus (covid-19) pandemic. The purpose of the letter was to provide banks with guidance on sound credit risk management policies and procedures to identify and measure credit risk in the context of coronavirus pandemic. 7.8.3 Calculation of expected credit losses Whether the impact of an event such as the coronavirus pandemic was reflected in an individual expected credit loss assessment (estimating the probability of default on an individual basis), factored into the scenario analysis of future macroeconomic conditions on a collective basis, or adjusted through management overlays, depended on the entity’s systems and processes and the facts and circumstances. In practice, entities were likely to consider a combination of these approaches. In estimating the impact of the event, entities needed to be careful to avoid double-counting the effects of the various assumptions applied in individual assessments, macroeconomic scenarios and management overlays. For the purpose of measuring expected credit loss and for determining whether a significant increase in credit risk had occurred, an entity grouped financial instruments on the basis of shared credit risk characteristics and reasonable and supportable information available on a portfolio basis. The occurrence of the coronavirus pandemic might have changed the risk characteristics of certain loans or receivables, because the respective borrowers or customers might have engaged in businesses, or been located in areas, which had become affected, or were more prone to be affected, by the pandemic. Therefore, entities considered (re)segmenting (sub)portfolios as appropriate. In abnormal circumstances such as those created by the coronavirus pandemic, it might take time before entities detect actual changes in risk indicators for a specific counterparty and be able to reassess the affected exposures. In order to accelerate the reflection of such changes in credit quality not yet detected at an individual borrower level, it might have been appropriate to adjust ratings and the probabilities of default on a collective basis, considering risk characteristics such as the industry or geographical location of the borrowers (see section 6.5 above). The impact of the coronavirus pandemic was not specific to banks. Corporates using a provision matrix approach to calculate expected credit loss for trade receivables would have needed to make appropriate adjustments to their historical loss rates and reflect the current economic environment as well as forward looking information. For example, a supplier of products or services to the airline industry would likely have had to consider that the probability of default (or loss rates, if a provision matrix approach is used) of its customers had increased irrespective of specific events identified at the level of individual counterparties. Many methods for performing collective assessments make use of historical information, which potentially are not relevant in the circumstances. Further, it might have been necessary to reconsider if loans needed to be segmented differently than in the past to reflect the risk characteristics. In estimating probability of default, loss rates and expected credit loss, entities considered the effect of any state aid plans to support customers through various measures (e.g., refinancing measures or other forms of financial support, including guarantees). Additionally, entities using multiple economic scenarios when estimating expected credit losses updated these scenarios to reflect the change in circumstances. Many financial institutions consider multiple macroeconomic scenarios in the assessment of expected credit losses (see section 6.7 above). In addition to updating gross domestic product expectations for the various scenarios, a challenge was to estimate how the impact of the coronavirus pandemic and any related government programmes affected specific sectors, regions and borrowers, especially as the details surrounding many government programmes continued to evolve. Financial instruments: Impairment The IASB educational document noted that a number of assumptions and linkages underlying the way expected credit losses had been implemented prior to the pandemic might no longer hold in such an environment. For example, the relationship between gross domestic product and other macroeconomic variables, such as unemployment and interest rates, and sector-specific variables, such as oil prices, was very likely to be different from what had been experienced in the past and reflected in economic forecasting models. The probability weightings assigned to macroeconomic scenarios might also need to have been revisited. Lenders also needed to assess how the value of collateral, such as the homes underlying mortgages or commercial property prices, would develop over the next few years. They would also need to take into account any guarantees from governments or other parties that were considered integral to the loans (see section 5.8.1.C above), having assessed the credit standing of the guarantor. In estimating overlays, entities might consider historical experience. However, it appears clear that the widespread nature and severity of the consequences of the coronavirus pandemic was not directly comparable with any recent similar events. It might have been appropriate for this purpose, to plot several possible scenarios of what might have happened over the forecasting period and assign weightings to them, to ensure that any overlay reflected the inherent uncertainty and non-linearity of potential outcomes. The IASB noted in their educational material that IFRS 9 requires the application of judgement and both requires and allows entities to adjust their approach to determining expected credit losses in different circumstances. 7.8.4 Determining whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk In the context of the coronavirus pandemic, one of the accounting issues was whether concessions made by lenders, such as changes in payment terms and the granting of payment holidays, would result in loans being move from stage 1 to stage 2. If payment terms were extended in light of the economic circumstances arising from the coronavirus pandemic, the terms and conditions of the extension would have been assessed to determine their impacts on the expected credit loss estimate as well as any other accounting impacts. For example, if the payment terms of a receivable were extended from 90 days to 180 days, this would likely not be considered a substantial modification of the receivable (see section 8 below). However, such an extension might result in an increase in probability of default, which would, in turn, affect the measurement of expected credit loss. For entities which did not apply the simplified model (see section 3.2 above), such extension might also result in moving the receivable to stage 2, depending on the extent and terms of the payment extension. However, if the same extension of payment terms was offered to an entire class of customers irrespective of individual circumstances, this generally would not result, by itself, in a stage movement. A number of assumptions and linkages underlying the way expected credit losses had been implemented prior to the pandemic might no longer hold in an environment affected by a pandemic or similar macroeconomic event. Entities should not have continued to apply their existing expected credit loss methodology mechanically. The accounting impact of relief measures on expected credit losses depended on the details of the arrangements. For example, the extension of payment holidays or a waiver of a breach of covenant to all borrowers in particular classes of financial instruments would not automatically result in all those instruments being viewed as having suffered a significant increase in credit risk. This would be the case even if a moratorium resulted in a loss for the lender (e.g., if interest payments were reduced or waived), if it was provided irrespective of the borrowers’ individual circumstances and the extension of payment holidays was assessed to be modifications rather than derecognition of the financial instruments (see section 8 below). In other situations, if relief measures were available only to those who met certain criteria, entities would need to carefully assess whether such criteria themselves might have indicated a significant increase in credit risk for the affected borrowers. For instance, a significant increase in credit risk was more likely to have occurred if a borrower applied for a relief measure which was available only to corporates which had suspended operations or individuals who had lost employment. Another example was if the relief, such as a deferral of loan payments, was offered to all participants in certain industries. This circumstance might indicate that borrowers in that industry were exposed to a higher risk of business failure and, thus, a higher probability of default as a class. In combination with other reasonable and supportable information, this was more likely to result in the classification of the related loans and other exposures in this portfolio, or a portion of them, into stage 2. The assessment of significant increase in credit risk is made irrespective of the fact that a concession is imposed by laws or regulations. Entities were also expected to exercise judgement, in light of all facts and circumstances, including the effect of government support, to determine if the respective loans were credit-impaired and were therefore classified as stage 3. Financial instruments: Impairment Regulators have stressed, in the context of the coronavirus epidemic, the need when assessing borrowers to differentiate a temporary liquidity need from a significant increase in credit risk and highlighted that there might have been very limited information available to make this determination at an individual borrower level. This means that lenders distinguished between obligors whose long-term credit risk was unlikely to be significantly affected by the pandemic from those who might be more permanently impacted. In light of the above, the 30 days past due backstop assumption might need to be rebutted in these circumstances (see section 6.2.2 above). Entities whose models included payment holidays or waiver of a covenant breach as an automatic significant increase in credit risk triggers might have needed to include overlays to unwind the effects if they determined that the significant increase in credit risk trigger was not warranted in this situation. For retail loans, data would often not be available to determine whether a significant increase in credit risk had occurred for individual borrowers. For wholesale exposures, more information was generally available on individual obligors, although the significant increase in credit risk assessment would still have been difficult. A lender might consider that borrowers in certain industries (e.g., airlines, tourism and hospitality) were exposed to a higher risk of business failure and, thus, an increased probability of default. When it was not practical to determine significant increase in credit risk on an individual basis, a collective approach to staging was considered (see section 6.5 above). This would also have been challenging. A possible method could have been to transfer to stage 2 a portion of those customers who had been given a payment holiday or a waiver of a covenant breach, whose probability of default was already close to the level that would trigger a significant increase in credit risk. Any approach would have required considerable judgement. Where additional rounds of relief measures were extended to existing borrowers, the same considerations which were applicable to assessing the initial relief were also applicable in determining whether the additional relief constituted a significant increase in credit risk. If the extension of the relief measures was offered only to selected borrowers (e.g., upon individual requests), it might have been harder to conclude that a significant increase in credit risk had not occurred, as the need for additional relief might be in response to further deterioration in the borrower’s financial position. 7.8.5 Disclosures Given the level of uncertainty and the sensitivity of judgements and estimates, the disclosure of the key assumptions used, and judgements made in estimating expected credit losses, as well as the concentration of risk, liquidity risk and the impact of any relief measures, were important (see section 15 below and # Chapter 50 at section 5.3). Important disclosures would have included, for example, the values of the key macroeconomic inputs used in the multiple economic scenario analysis and the probability weights of these scenarios, as well as the assumptions used to determine how the different challenges for specific sectors and regions had been taken into account and the effect of any management overlays. In addition, entities provided disclosures to allow users of financial statements to understand the nature of any material reliefs offered to their borrowers, including those enforced by governments, and how they had assessed whether they constituted forbearance, whether they resulted in a substantial modification of the contract, their effect on staging and the impact on the overall expected credit loss. Entities also disclosed any material form of government grant or government assistance received in accordance with IAS 20. Entities also considered any guidance and expectations on disclosures of expected credit losses issued by prudential and securities regulators in their jurisdictions. How we see it The assessment of the impact of the coronavirus pandemic and recovery on expected credit losses required significant judgement, especially as it was not directly comparable with any recent similar events and the impact depended on government measures as much as the spread of the virus. Entities would have updated their macroeconomic scenarios and considered the use of top-down ‘management overlays’ to embed in the expected credit loss risks not otherwise fully captured by their models or to avoid overstating expected credit losses. Given the level of uncertainty and the sensitivity of judgements and estimates, disclosures of the key assumptions used and judgements made in estimating expected credit losses, as well as the impact of any relief measures, was important. Financial instruments: Impairment To promote consistency and enable comparability of the expected credit loss estimate in those instances, banks were expected to provide additional quantitative and qualitative disclosures. The disclosures banks presented included the following, where relevant: • An explanation of the nature of the adjustments and how these were calculated for each material overlay and ‘in’ or ‘post’ model adjustment. • The reasons for the adjustment and impact, specifically what risks it addressed, including whether it was made to incorporate scenarios that could not be modelled without undue cost or effort (for example, to incorporate the impact of certain forms of government support or to include the most recent macroeconomic information available), to address model limitations (for example, the fact that the model results were based on outdated correlations or assumptions), adjust the expected increase in delinquency currently concealed by moratoria or reflect increased sectorial idiosyncrasies. • Quantitative analysis, including, as a minimum, the absolute size of the adjustment, the overall effect on the expected credit loss estimate resulting from it, especially if materially changed compared to the last year-end, and narrative to explain the movements and expectations on timing of withdrawal going forward. Alternatively, the impact could have been disclosed as a percentage of the overall expected credit loss. • Allocation by product, business segment and type of overlay (model adjustments due to models working outside historical observations, uncertainty overlays, sector related overlays, etc.), highlighting possible offsetting effects between different types of overlays (for example, between an overlay applied for potential default suppression due to government support schemes and an overlay applied to adjust probability of defaults when models were working outside historical observations). How we see it A number of banks provided quantitative and qualitative information in respect of post-model adjustments and management overlays while others merely mentioned that total expected credit losses included these elements without providing further analysis. Disclosures provided were diverse, with some banks breaking down these elements into the segments/portfolios/products they related to, while others referred to them without providing a numerical analysis or merely providing a total. Given the significant size of the model adjustments and management overlays, we would expect increased transparency and granularity in the relevant disclosures and that such disclosures are sufficiently detailed and clear for the users of the financial statements to understand. Financial instruments: Impairment An example of disclosures dealing with the impact of the coronavirus pandemic on expected credit losses, methodology, critical estimates and judgements, how economic scenarios were reflected in expected credit loss calculations and management judgemental adjustments to the expected credit loss of a bank is illustrated in Practical example 7-1 below. Practical example 7-1: HSBC Holdings plc (2022) United Kingdom Annual Report and Accounts 2022 [extract] Risk review [extract] Credit risk [extract] … Financial instruments: Impairment … Financial instruments: Impairment … Financial instruments: Impairment Financial instruments: Impairment … ## Contents - 3. Critical accounting judgements and key sources of estimation uncertainty [extract] Financial instruments: Impairment Another example of disclosure dealing with the impact of the coronavirus pandemic on expected credit losses of a corporate is illustrated in Practical example 7-2 below. Practical example 7-2: Centrica plc (2021) United Kingdom Annual Report and Accounts 2021 [extract] Notes to the Financial Statements [extract] … 7.9 Accounting for climate change Climate change may affect an entity’s assessment of expected credit losses, potentially leading to higher allowances for expected credit losses. The longer the term of the exposures and the greater the extent to which the counterparty is likely to be affected by climate change, the greater this effect is likely to be. Moreover, there is also the risk of climate change related rare shock events that needs to be taken into account, e.g., severe drought or heavy rainfall causing severe flooding, potentially resulting in damage to crops and homes, rising food prices and driving individuals and businesses into financial difficulties (see section 5.6.3 above) which could occur in the short term. Financial instruments: Impairment IFRS 9 requires use of forward-looking information to recognise expected credit losses. There is a variety of increasingly possible adverse future climate risk economic scenarios that could impact the probability of borrowers defaulting and the extent of losses that lenders may incur in the event of default. Climate risks are broadly categorised into physical and transition risks: • Physical risks include the risk of loss due to specific weather events (such as storms or wildfires) and due to longer-term changes in weather trends (such as rising sea levels). Physical risks can impact the creditworthiness of borrowers due to business interruption, impacts on economic strength, asset values and unemployment. • Transition risks relate to the risk of financial loss due to the economic transition toward a more sustainable economy. Transition risks could also result in a rapid deterioration of credit quality in sectors and/or countries affected, particularly if policy changes are radical or quickly implemented, and these factors are considered in a borrower’s ability to repay and service debt. From a credit risk perspective, physical and transition risks related to climate change could potentially impact: • Probability of default affects staging of exposures and the measurement of expected credit loss allowances. Historical correlations that predict defaults may no longer be relevant. Methods for risk rating customers may, therefore, need to be updated as new financial and non-financial metrics capturing the impacts of climate change are made available. The impact of regulatory actions should also be considered. • Loss given default affects the ultimate measurement of the expected credit loss allowance. Physical and transition risks may affect collateral values, for example, on mortgage loans, and lead to entities employing different collection strategies for distressed debt. • Entities may make use of judgemental in-model or post-model adjustments to incorporate climate risks into their expected credit loss allowances, particularly in the short term due to data and model limitations. Enhanced governance processes will be required to support these judgements. • Entities may need to incorporate climate risk factors into their macroeconomic scenarios and forward- looking information. The impact of climate risks on these scenarios is likely to be greater, the longer the duration of the underlying exposures and the greater an entity’s exposure is to vulnerable sectors or geographies. • Climate risk is likely to increase concentration risk exposures in vulnerable sectors or geographies. Sectors like agriculture and insurance may be particularly vulnerable to physical risks. For example, mining and oil and gas may be particularly exposed to transition risk. Some geographies such as those at or close to sea level or exposed to drought or flooding may be particularly exposed to physical risks. Entities will need to consider high risk sectors, industries, portfolios or geographies which require a collective staging assessment, e.g., high risk industries for climate change, mortgages in high flood-risk area and loans to the energy sector. Also, changes in risk concentrations may affect the modelling of expected credit loss allowances and the disclosure of credit risk concentrations. Entities, particularly those with large credit risk exposures like banks, are in various stages of integrating climate risks into their risk frameworks, and there exist several challenges in this regard: • Climate-related risks are uncertain, non-linear and pervasive. • There are multiple projections and scenarios available to understand the size and impact of climate risk, but limited projections for economic and financial effects. • Data and projections available are not always comparable, given divergence in taxonomies and standards globally. • Entities need to collect new types of data on their customers and put in place new processes and governance. • Identifying the right metrics to measure climate risk exposure can be challenging. • Clarity or consensus on incorporating climate scenario analysis into risk assessments is still emerging. This is an area which is likely to develop as the increased publication of sustainability reports will result in more data being available for use in ECL modelling. This is an area which attracts the attention of regulators and is likely to continue to do so. Regulators are also focussing on the connectivity between accounting and sustainability standards. Practical example 7-3 shows how National Australia Bank Limited considered its exposure to climate risks as part of its credit risk assessment in its 2023 annual financial statements. In this example, the exposure to environmental, social and governance risks have been captured as part of a forward-looking adjustment. Financial instruments: Impairment Practical example 7-3: National Australia Bank Limited (2023) Australia ANNUAL FINANCIAL REPORT 2023 [extract] NOTES TO THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] NOTE 19 FINANCIAL RISK MANAGEMENT [extract] Credit risk [extract] Practical example 7-4 shows how ABN AMRO Bank N.V. accounted for the potential transition risk by including a management overlay in the ECL calculation. Practical example 7-4: ABN AMRO Bank N.V. (2023) Netherlands INTEGRATED ANNUAL REPORT 2023 [extract] Risk, funding & capital [extract] Risk management [extract] Credit risk review [extract] Individual and collective loan impairment allowances and management overlays [extract] … … Financial instruments: Impairment In its 2023 Annual report, UBS Group AG disclosed the impact of sustainability and climate risk on the measurement of ECL. As shown in Practical example 7-5, while the impact was not material, the reasons for this outcome were disclosed. Practical example 7-5: UBS Group AG (2023) Switzerland Annual Report 2023 [extract] ## Financial statements [extract] Consolidated financial statements [extract] UBS Group AG consolidated financial statements [extract] Notes to the UBS Group AG consolidated financial statements [extract] Additional information [extract] Note 20 Expected credit loss measurement [extract] f) Sensitivity information [extract] … In its 2023 Annual report NatWest Group plc disclosed it was including climate related assumptions into its macroeconomic model and quantified an ECL impact. Practical example 7-6: NatWest Group plc (2023) United Kingdom ## Annual Report and Accounts [extract] Risk and Capital Management [extract] Credit Risk [extract] Economic Loss Drivers [extract] … It is important to note that the factors outlined here should be considered by any entity that holds financial assets that are in the scope of the IFRS 9 impairment requirements - this includes financial institutions, as well as corporates. For example, Practical example 7-6 discloses how Rolls-Royce plc considered the impact of potentially vulnerable sectors when estimating ECL. Financial instruments: Impairment Practical example 7-7: Rolls-Royce Holdings plc (2023) United Kingdom Annual Report 2023 [extract] FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] ## Accounting policies [extract] Climate change [extract] … How we see it The effect of climate risk on an entity’s expected credit loss calculations is likely to be greater the longer the duration of the underlying exposures and the greater the entity’s exposure to vulnerable sectors or geographies, although physical risks may also lead to short term exposures. Given the judgemental nature of incorporating climate risks into expected credit loss calculations, it is important that entities establish strong governance processes to support material judgements that they make in this regard, and that there are enhanced disclosures made about the risks and the impact of significant judgements and estimation uncertainty. Financial instruments: Impairment Modified financial assets If the contractual cash flows on a financial asset are renegotiated or modified, the holder needs to assess whether the financial asset is derecognised (see Chapter 48 at section 3.5 and Chapter 46 at section 3.8 for further details on modification and derecognition). In summary, an entity should derecognise a financial asset if the cash flows are extinguished or if the terms of the instrument have substantially changed. This applies to all modifications whether they result from commercial or other reasons that are unrelated to credit risk. Figure 8-1: Application of the derecognition and modification requirements and their interaction with expected credit loss assessment 8.1 Accounting treatment if modified financial assets are derecognised In some circumstances, the renegotiation or modification of the contractual cash flows of a financial asset can lead to the derecognition of the existing financial asset and subsequently, the recognition of a new financial asset. [IFRS 9.B5.5.25]. This means that the entity is starting fresh and the date of the modification will also be the date of initial recognition of the new financial asset at its fair value. Typically, the entity will recognise a loss allowance based on 12-month expected credit losses at each reporting date until the requirements for the recognition of lifetime expected credit losses are met. However, in what IFRS 9 describes as ‘some unusual circumstances’ following a modification that results in derecognition of the original financial asset, there may be evidence that the new financial asset is credit-impaired on initial recognition (see section 3.3 above). Thus, the financial asset is recognised as an originated credit-impaired financial asset. [IFRS 9.B5.5.26]. Financial instruments: Impairment 8.2 Accounting treatment if modified financial assets are not derecognised In other circumstances, the renegotiation or modification of the contractual cash flows of a financial asset does not lead to the derecognition of the existing financial asset under IFRS 9. In such situations, the entity will: • Continue with its current accounting treatment for the existing asset that has been modified. • Recognise a modification gain or loss in profit or loss by recalculating the gross carrying amount of the financial asset as the present value of the renegotiated or modified contractual cash flows, discounted at the financial asset’s original effective interest rate (or the credit-adjusted effective interest rate for purchased or originated credit-impaired financial assets). Any costs or fees incurred adjust the carrying amount of the modified financial asset and are amortised over the remaining term of the modified financial asset (see section 3.1 above). [IFRS 9.5.4.3, IFRS 9 Appendix A]. • Assess whether there has been a significant increase in the credit risk of the financial instrument, by comparing the risk of a default occurring at the reporting date (based on the modified contractual terms) and the risk of a default occurring at initial recognition (based on the original, unmodified contractual terms). A financial asset that has been renegotiated or modified is not automatically considered to have lower credit risk. The assessment considers the credit risk over the expected life of the asset based on historical and forward-looking information, including information about the circumstances that led to the modification. Evidence that the criteria for the recognition of lifetime expected credit losses are subsequently no longer met may include a history of up-to-date and timely payment in subsequent periods. This means a minimum period of observation will often be necessary before a financial asset may qualify to return to stage 1. [IFRS 9.5.5.12, IFRS 9.B5.5.27]. • Make the appropriate quantitative and qualitative disclosures required for renegotiated or modified assets to enable users of financial statements to understand the nature and effect of such modifications (including the effect on the measurement of expected credit losses) and how the entity monitors its assets that have been modified (see section 15 below and Chapter 50 at section 5.3). [IFRS 7.35F(f), IFRS 7.B8B, IFRS 7.35J]. If a financial asset is modified as part of forbearance and the modification results in forgiveness of part of the contractual cash flows, the entity might need to consider whether any portion of the financial asset should be written off before the modification takes place. The following Illustration has been adapted from Example 11 of the Illustrative Examples to IFRS 9 to illustrate the accounting treatment of a loan that is modified. It is noted that it does not consider whether any of the contractual cash flows are written off, as a partial derecognition. [IFRS 9.IE Example 11, IFRS 9.IE66- IE73]. Illustration 8-1: Modification of contractual cash flows Bank A originates a five-year loan that requires the repayment of the outstanding contractual amount in full at maturity. Its contractual par amount is CU1,000 with an interest rate of 5%, payable annually. The effective interest rate is 5%. At the end of the first reporting period in Year 1, Bank A recognises a loss allowance at an amount equal to 12-month expected credit losses because there has not been a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition. A loss allowance balance of CU20 is recognised. In Year 2, Bank A determines that the credit risk on the loan has increased significantly since initial recognition. As a result, Bank A recognises lifetime expected credit losses on the loan. The loss allowance balance is CU150 at the end of year 2. At the end of Year 3, following significant financial difficulty of the borrower, Bank A modifies the contractual cash flows on the loan. It forgoes interest payments beyond year 3 until maturity and extends the contractual term of the loan by one year so that the remaining term at the date of the modification is three years. The modification does not result in the derecognition of the loan by Bank A. As a result of that modification, Bank A recalculates the gross carrying amount of the financial asset as the present value of the modified contractual cash flows discounted at the loan’s original effective interest rate of 5%. The difference between this recalculated gross carrying amount and the gross carrying amount before the modification is recognised as a modification gain or loss. Bank A recognises the modification loss (calculated as CU136) against the gross carrying amount of the loan, reducing it to CU864, and a modification loss of CU136 in profit or loss. Bank A also remeasures the loss allowance, taking into account the modified contractual cash flows and evaluates whether the loss allowance for the loan continues to be measured at an amount equal to lifetime expected credit losses. Bank A compares the current credit risk (taking into consideration the modified cash flows) to the credit risk (on the original unmodified cash flows) at initial recognition. Bank A determines that Financial instruments: Impairment the loan is not credit-impaired at the reporting date but that credit risk has still significantly increased compared to the credit risk at initial recognition. It continues to measure the loss allowance at an amount equal to the lifetime expected credit loss, which is CU110 at the reporting date. At each subsequent reporting date, Bank A continues to evaluate whether there has been a significant increase in credit risk by comparing the loan’s credit risk at initial recognition (based on the original, unmodified cash flows) with the credit risk at the reporting date (based on the modified cash flows). Two reporting periods after the loan modification (Year 5), the borrower has outperformed its business plan significantly compared to the expectations at the modification date. In addition, the outlook for the business is more positive than previously envisaged. An assessment of all reasonable and supportable information that is available without undue cost or effort indicates that the overall credit risk on the loan has decreased and that the risk of a default occurring over the expected life of the loan has decreased, so Bank A adjusts the borrower’s internal credit rating at the end of the reporting period. Given the positive overall development, Bank A re-assesses the situation and concludes that the credit risk of the loan has decreased and there is no longer a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition. As a result, Bank A once again measures the loss allowance at an amount equal to 12-month expected credit losses. Year Beginning gross carrying amount CU Impairment (loss)/gain CU Modification (loss)/gain CU Interest revenue CU Cash flows CU Ending gross carrying amount CU Loss allowance CU Ending amortised cost amount CU A B C D Gross: A × 5% E F = A + **C + D - E** G **H = F - G** 1,000 (20) 1,000 1,000 (130) 1,000 1,000 (136) 1,000 Frequently asked questions Question 8-1: How to determine the expected credit losses in respect of a modified financial asset if the modification had not resulted in a derecognition but the modified cash flows have been renegotiated in such a way as to represent the cash flows which the lender expects the borrower can repay? (IFRS Transition Resource Group for Impairment of Financial Instruments meeting 22 April 2015 - Agenda paper no. 8) At its meeting on 22 April 2015, the IFRS Transition Resource Group for Impairment of Financial Instruments members (see section 1.4 above) noted that IFRS 9 is clear that an entity is required to calculate a new gross carrying amount and the gain or loss on modification taken to profit or loss is based on the renegotiated or modified contractual cash flows and excludes expected credit losses unless it is a purchased or originated credit-impaired financial asset. [IFRS 9.5.4.3, IFRS 9 Appendix A]. Consequently, an entity must calculate the gain or loss on modification as a first step before going on to consider the revised expected credit loss allowance required on the modified financial asset. Thereafter, the entity is required to continue to apply the impairment requirements to the modified financial asset in the same way as it would for other unmodified financial instruments, taking into account the revised contractual terms. [IFRS 9.5.5.12]. The revised expected credit loss cannot be assumed to be nil as, in accordance with IFRS 9.5.5.18, an entity is required to consider the possibility that a credit loss occurs, even if the likelihood of that credit loss occurring is very low. [IFRS 9.5.5.18]. The IFRS Transition Resource Group for Impairment of Financial Instruments also discussed the appropriate presentation and disclosure requirements pertaining to modifications. These are discussed further in Chapter 50 at section 7.1.1. Financial instruments: Impairment How we see it If an entity has no reasonable expectations of recovering a portion of the financial asset, which is subsequently forgiven, then this amount is arguably written off, as a partial derecognition. The gross carrying amount would be reduced directly before a modification gain or loss is calculated. This would mean that the loss will be recorded as an impairment loss, rather than as a loss on modification, and presented differently in the profit or loss account. In practice, it will often be difficult to disentangle the effects of modification and write-off, as some forgone cash flows may be compensated for by a higher interest rate applied to the remaining contractual amounts due. Financial instruments: Impairment Financial assets measured at fair value through other comprehensive income For financial assets measured at fair value through other comprehensive income (see Chapter 44 at section 4.3), the expected credit losses do not reduce the carrying amount of the financial assets in the statement of financial position, which remains at fair value. Instead, an amount equal to the allowance that would arise if the asset was measured at amortised cost is recognised in other comprehensive income as the ‘accumulated impairment amount’. [IFRS 9.4.1.2A, IFRS 9.5.5.2, IFRS 9 Appendix A]. 9.1 Accounting treatment for debt instruments measured at fair value through other comprehensive income The accounting treatment and journal entries for debt instruments measured at fair value through other comprehensive income are shown in the following Illustration, based on Illustrative Example 13 in the Illustrative Examples to IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.IE Example 13, IFRS 9.IE78, IFRS 9.IE79, IFRS 9.IE80, IFRS 9.IE81]. Illustration 9-1: Debt instrument measured at fair value through other comprehensive income An entity purchases a debt instrument with a fair value of CU1,000 on 15 December 20X2 and measures the debt instrument at fair value through other comprehensive income. The instrument has an interest rate of 5% over the contractual term of 10 years, and has a 5% effective interest rate. At initial recognition the entity determines that the asset is not purchased or originated credit-impaired. Dr Financial asset - fair value through other comprehensive income CU1,000 Cr Cash CU1,000 To recognise the debt instrument measured at its fair value On 31 December 20X2 (the reporting date), the fair value of the debt instrument has decreased to CU950 as a result of changes in market interest rates. The entity determines that there has not been a significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition and that expected credit losses should be measured at an amount equal to 12-month expected credit losses, which amounts to CU30. For simplicity, journal entries for the receipt of interest revenue are not provided. Dr Impairment loss (profit or loss) CU30 Dr Other comprehensive income(a) CU20 Cr Financial asset - fair value through other comprehensive income CU50 To recognise 12-month expected credit losses and other fair value changes on the debt instrument (a) The cumulative loss in other comprehensive income at the reporting date was CU20. That amount consists of the total fair value change of CU50 (i.e., CU1,000 - CU950) offset by the change in the accumulated impairment amount representing 12-month expected credit losses that was recognised (CU30). Disclosure would be provided about the accumulated impairment amount of CU30. On 1 January 20X3, the entity decides to sell the debt instrument for CU950, which is its fair value at that date. Dr Cash CU950 Cr Financial asset - fair value through other comprehensive income CU950 Dr Loss (profit or loss) CU20 Cr Other comprehensive income CU20 To derecognise the fair value through other comprehensive income asset and recycle amounts accumulated in other comprehensive income to profit or loss, i.e., CU20 This means that in contrast to financial assets measured at amortised cost, there is no separate allowance but, instead, impairment gains or losses are accounted for as an adjustment of the revaluation reserve accumulated in other comprehensive income, with a corresponding charge to profit or loss (which is then reflected in retained earnings). The tax implications of debt instruments measured at fair value through other comprehensive income is discussed further in Chapter 29 at sections 10.4.1 and 10.4.2. Financial instruments: Impairment As explained in section 7.3.1 above, IFRS 9 requires a derecognition gain or loss to be measured relative to the carrying amount at the date of derecognition. This necessitates an assessment and measurement of expected credit losses for that particular asset as at the date of derecognition. 9.2 Interaction between foreign currency translation, fair value hedge accounting and impairment The above example is relatively straightforward. A more complicated one, based on a foreign currency denominated financial asset which is also the subject of an interest rate hedge, is provided below. It is based on Illustrative Example 14 in the Illustrative Examples to IFRS 9 but has been adjusted so as to include the effect of discounting in the measurement of expected credit losses (see section 5.7 above). [IFRS 9.IE82-IE102]. Note that we do not address the additional complexities that will arise from the consideration of taxation, including deferred tax. The tax accounting implications of debt instruments measured at fair value through other comprehensive income is discussed further in Chapter 29 at sections 10.4.1 and 10.4.2. Illustration 9-2: Interaction between the fair value through other comprehensive income measurement category and foreign currency denomination, fair value hedge accounting and impairment The example assumes the following fact pattern and that, on initial recognition, expected credit losses are included when measuring foreign exchange gains and losses (see section 7.3.1 above): • An entity purchases a bond denominated in a foreign currency (FC) for its fair value of FC100,000 on 1 January 20X2. • The bond is held within a business model whose objective is achieved by both collecting contractual cash flows and selling financial assets and has contractual cash flows which are solely payments of principal and interest on the principal amount outstanding. Therefore, the entity classifies the bond as measured at fair value through other comprehensive income. • The bond has five years remaining to maturity and a fixed coupon of 5% over its contractual life on the contractual par amount of FC100,000. • The entity hedges the bond for its interest rate related fair value risk. The fair value of the corresponding interest rate swap at the date of initial recognition is nil. • On initial recognition, the bond has a 5% effective interest rate which results in a gross carrying amount that equals the fair value at initial recognition. • The entity’s functional currency is its local currency (LC). • As at 1 January 20X2, the exchange rate is FC1 to LC1. • At initial recognition, the entity determines that the bond is not purchased credit-impaired. The entity applies a 12-month probability of default for its impairment calculation and assumes that payment default occurs at the end of the reporting period (i.e., after 12 months). In particular, the entity estimates the probability of default over the next 12 months at 2% and the loss given default at FC60,000, resulting in an (undiscounted) expected cash shortfall of FC1,200. The discounted expected cash shortfall is FC1,143 at 5% effective interest rate (see the example below for the detailed calculation). • For simplicity, amounts for interest revenue are not provided. It is assumed that interest accrued is received in the period. Differences of 1 in the calculations and reconciliations are due to rounding. The entity hedges its risk exposures using the following risk management strategy: (a) For fixed interest rate risk (in FC) the entity decides to link its interest receipts in FC to current variable interest rates in FC. Consequently, the entity uses interest rate swaps denominated in FC under which it pays fixed interest and receives variable interest in FC. (b) For foreign exchange (FX) risk, the entity decides not to hedge against any variability in LC arising from changes in foreign exchange rates. The entity designates the following hedging relationship: a fair value hedge of the bond in FC as the hedged item with changes in benchmark interest rate risk in FC as the hedged risk. The entity enters into a swap that pays fixed and receives variable interest in FC on the same day and designates the swap as the hedging instrument. The tenor of the swap matches that of the hedged item (i.e., five years). This example assumes that all qualifying criteria for hedge accounting are met (see IFRS 9.6.4.1). The description of the designation is solely for the purpose of understanding this example (i.e., it is not an example of the complete formal documentation required in accordance with IFRS 9.6.4.1). Financial instruments: Impairment This example assumes that no hedge ineffectiveness arises in the hedging relationship. This assumption is made in order to better focus on illustrating the accounting mechanics in a situation that entails measurement at fair value through other comprehensive income of a foreign currency financial instrument that is designated in a fair value hedge relationship, and also to focus on the recognition of impairment gains or losses on such an instrument. The entity decided not to amortise the fair value hedge adjustment to profit or loss before the hedge ceases or the asset is credit-impaired. Consequently, in this example, there is no adjustment to the effective interest rate due to fair value hedge accounting. However, such an adjustment to the effective interest rate would at the latest be required when the entity ceases to apply hedge accounting or when the asset becomes credit- impaired, i.e., moved to stage 3. (See section 7.5 above). Situation as per 1 January 20X2 The table below illustrates the amounts recognised in the financial statements as per 1 January 20X2, as well as the shadow amortised cost calculation for the bond, based on the fact pattern described above (debits are shown as positive numbers and credits as negative numbers): Financial Statements Shadow Calculation FC LC FC LC Statement of financial position Bond (FV) 100,000 100,000 Gross carrying amount 100,000 100,000 Swap (FV) - - Loss allowance (1,143) (1,143) Amortised cost 98,857 98,857 Statement of profit or loss Impairment 1,143 1,143 FV hedge adjustment - - FV hedge (bond) - - Adjusted gross carrying amt. 100,000 100,000 FX gain/loss (bond) - - Adjusted amortised cost 98,857 98,857 FV hedge (swap) - - FX gain/loss (swap) - - Statement of other comprehensive income FV changes - - Impairment offset (1,143) (1,143) FV hedge adjustment - - As per 1 January 20X2, the entity recognises the bond and the swap at their initial fair values of LC100,000 and nil, respectively. The loss allowance of FC1,143 is recognised in profit or loss. The amount is calculated as the difference between all contractual cash flows that are due to the entity in accordance with the contract and all the cash flows that the entity expects to receive (i.e., all cash shortfalls), discounted at the original effective interest of 5%, and weighted by the probability of the scenario occurring. To keep the example simple, it is assumed that default on the bond occurs one year after the date of the initial recognition, at which point the recoverable amount of the bond is received. This means that in the case of a default the entity expects cash flows of FC45,000 (which is the principal of FC100,000 plus one year of interest of FC5,000 less the loss given default of FC60,000). The latter loss is discounted by the 5% effective interest rate and weighted by the 2% probability of default to arrive at the loss allowance.
